On the well-posedness of the intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the line
Andreia Chapouto, Justin Forlano, Thierry Laurens

Abstract
. We consider a family of intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equations (INLS) on the real line, which includes the continuum Calogero-Moser models (CCM). We prove that INLS is locally well-posed in H s ​ ( ℝ ) H^{s}(\mathbb{R}) for any s > 1 4 s>\frac{1}{4} , which improves upon the previous best result of s > 1 2 s>\frac{1}{2} by de Moura-Pilod (2008). This result is also new in the special case of CCM, as the initial condition is not required to lie in any Hardy space. Our approach is based on a gauge transformation, exploiting the remarkable structure of the nonlinearity together with bilinear Strichartz estimates, which allows to recover some of the derivative loss. This turns out to be sufficient to establish our main results for CCM in the Hardy space. For INLS and CCM outside of the Hardy space, the main difficulty comes from the lack of the Hardy space assumption, which we overcome by implementing a refined decomposition of the solutions, which observes a nonlinear smoothing effect in part of the solution. We also discover a new Lax pair for INLS and use it to establish global well-posedness in H s ​ ( ℝ ) H^{s}(\mathbb{R}) for any s > 1 4 s>\frac{1}{4} under the additional assumption of small L 2 L^{2} -norm.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35A01, 35Q35, 35Q55, 37K10

1.
Introduction

We consider the Cauchy problem for the
intermediate nonlinear Schrödinger equation (INLS):

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=\beta u(1+i\mathcal{T}_{h})\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u,\\
&u|_{t=0}=u_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

where
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
,
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\mathcal{T}_{h}
is the singular integral operator with kernel

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}f(x)=\frac{1}{2h}\text{p.v.}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\coth\bigg(\frac{\pi(x-y)}{2h}\bigg)f(y)dy,\quad 0<h<\infty,

(1.2)

where
p.v.
denotes the principal value. By taking the Fourier transform, we see that
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is a Fourier multiplier operator with multiplier

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\{\mathcal{T}_{h}f\}(\xi)=-i\coth(h\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

(1.3)

The INLS equation (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
was derived by Pelinovsky
[
51
]
as a model for the evolution of quasi-harmonic internal waves in a two fluid layer system, where the bottom fluid is of a finite depth
h>0
. In the same work, Pelinovsky demonstrated the existence of multi-soliton solutions, strongly indicating that (
1.1
) is completely integrable. This was verified by Pelinovsky-Grimshaw
[
52
]
where they developed the inverse scattering transform, used it to explain the multi-solitons, and found an infinite sequence of conservation laws.
The scattering transform (and Lax pair) in
[
52
]
involves
2\times 2
matrix operators, which can be heuristically explained since in the shallow-depth limit
(h\to 0)
, and after a suitable change of variables, (
1.1
) formally converges to the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation.
Later, Pelinovsky-Grimshaw
[
53
]
took into account higher order effects leading to (
1.1
) for
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Regarding the well-posedness theory for INLS (
1.1
), little appears to be known.
An important aspect of this is to determine scaling critical spaces, which suggest where the barrier to well-posedness lies.
Given
\lambda\geq 1
and a smooth solution
u
to (
1.1
), the rescaled solution
u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}u(\lambda^{-2}t,\lambda^{-1}x)

satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{\lambda}+i\partial_{x}^{2}u_{\lambda}=2\beta u_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{h\lambda}\partial_{x}(|u_{\lambda}|^{2})+i\lambda^{-1}\gamma|u_{\lambda}|^{2}u_{\lambda}

with initial data
u_{0,\lambda}(x)=u_{0}(\lambda^{-1}x)
. When
\gamma=0
, the scaling is not an exact symmetry; rather, the family of equations (
1.1
) with depth parameters
0<h<\infty
remains invariant under scaling.
This scaling then reveals that
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the scaling critical space. When
\gamma\neq 0
, the contribution from the term
|u|^{2}u
scales favourably so we still expect criticality in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
In this direction, de Moura
[
16
]
established the local well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s\geq 1
and for small initial data. Using a gauge transformation, de Moura-Pilod
[
17
]
proved local well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s>\frac{1}{2}
and removed the small data restriction. Later, Barros-de Moura-Santos
[
6
]
proved local well-posedness for (
1.1
) with sufficiently small initial data in the Besov space
B^{\frac{1}{2}}_{2,1}(\mathbb{R})
.
It was shown in
[
50
]
that, if it exists, the solution map cannot be
C^{3}
at the origin in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s<0
.
One of the main goals of this
article is to go beyond the results of
[
17
,
6
]
and make progress towards the scaling critical space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
without using complete integrability. This opens up handling perturbations of (
1.1
) such as the physically relevant case with
\gamma\neq 0
, which may not be completely integrable.

For fixed
\xi\neq 0
, from (
1.3
), we see that
-i\coth(h\xi)\to-i\textup{sgn}(\xi)
, which we recognise as the Fourier multiplier associated to the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
. Thus, by formally taking
h\to\infty
in (
1.1
) we arrive at our second equation of interest in this paper:

\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=\beta u(1+i\mathcal{H})\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u.

(1.4)

This discussion suggests defining
\mathcal{T}_{\infty}=\mathcal{H}
, extending (
1.1
) to the case when
h=\infty
.
We rewrite (
1.4
) into a more familiar form. Given a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we write
\mathbf{1}_{A}
to be the characteristic function of the set
A
. Then, with
\mathbf{P}_{\pm}
defined as the Fourier multiplier operator with symbol
\mathbf{1}_{\{\pm\xi>0\}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{+}+\mathbf{P}_{-}=\text{Id}

(1.5)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=-i\mathbf{P}_{+}+i\mathbf{P}_{-}\quad\text{so that}\quad 1+i\mathcal{H}=2\mathbf{P}_{+}.

(1.6)

Inserting the second identity in (
1.6
) into (
1.4
), we arrive at the equation:

\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u.

(1.7)

When
\gamma=0
, we recognise (
1.7
) as the continuum Calogero-Moser equation (CCM):

\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}).

(1.8)

The sign of
\beta
determines the type of CCM we consider: defocusing if
\beta>0
or focusing if
\beta<0
. The defocusing and focusing varieties arise in unique physical contexts. The defocusing CCM (
1.8
) was derived by Pelinovsky
[
51
]
as the infinite depth limit of (
1.1
), whereas the focusing CCM (
1.8
) was formally derived in
[
1
]
as a continuum limit of classical Calogero-Moser particle systems.

Remarkably, the infinite-depth model (
1.8
) formally leaves the Hardy space
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
invariant (see (
2.2
)).
More precisely, if
u_{0}\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, then the same is true for the solution
u
. Indeed, by the Fourier convolution theorem, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\{u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})\}(\xi)=\int_{\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}}i(\xi_{3}-\xi_{2})\mathbf{1}_{\{\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}>0\}}\widehat{u}(\xi_{1})\overline{\widehat{u}(\xi_{2})}\widehat{u}(\xi_{3})d\xi_{1}d\xi_{2}.

(1.9)

If we assume that
\mathbf{P}_{+}u=u
, then the projection
\mathbf{P}_{+}
, the fact that
\xi_{1}>0
and the hyperplane condition
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}
imply that
\xi>0
, showing that the nonlinearity in (
1.8
) preserves the Hardy space assumption.
We point out that whilst both focusing and defocusing CCM (
1.8
) preserve the Hardy space, only the focusing variety was derived in the context of solutions in
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
in
[
1
]
; Pelinovky’s derivation of CCM in the defocusing case
[
51
]
does not impose a Hardy space assumption.
More remarkably, CCM on
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
is completely integrable, and a Lax pair was observed by Gérard-Lenzmann
[
22
]
.
In their influential paper, they established the local well-posedness in
H^{s}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
(see (
2.2
)), investigated the global well-posedness and construction of multi-solitons, and demonstrated that the focusing CCM displays turbulent behaviour.
In particular, they observed that there is a threshold for global solutions to the focusing CCM (
1.8
) in the Hardy space due to the unique static solution

\displaystyle u(t,x)=\mathcal{R}(x)=\tfrac{\sqrt{2}}{x+i}\in H^{1}_{+}(\mathbb{R})

(1.10)

which has mass
M(\mathcal{R})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\mathcal{R}|^{2}dx=2\pi
. Here
\mathcal{R}
is the unique (up to symmetries) ground state for the energy
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x}u-i\mathbf{P}_{+}(|u|^{2})u|^{2}dx.

The value
M(\mathcal{R})
is then the discriminator for when the conservation laws fail to control the
H^{s}
-norms of solutions. For
s\geq 1
,
[
22
]
showed that solutions exists globally-in-time if
M(u_{0})\leq M(\mathcal{R})
, with an emphasis on the equality case here.
This threshold has since been shown to be sharp
[
27
,
32
]
.
We mention that the inverse scattering theory for CCM in the defocusing
[
44
]
and focusing
[
19
]
cases has also been studied, as well as for defocusing INLS
[
52
]
.
Much attention has also been given to special solutions of these models, including the defocusing
[
3
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
43
,
42
,
45
]
and focusing
[
3
,
46
]
CCM equations, and the defocusing
[
51
]
and focusing
[
59
]
INLS equations.

As for low-regularity well-posedness,
the third author with Killip and Vişan
[
30
]
proved global well-posedness of CCM (
1.8
) in the scaling critical space
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
for the defocusing version of (
1.8
) and for the focusing version below
M(\mathcal{R})
.
Their argument crucially exploits the complete integrability of (
1.8
) and an explicit formula for solutions in the Hardy space akin to that first discovered by Gérard
[
20
]
for the Benjamin-Ono equation (BO).
Other interesting recent developments include investigating the zero dispersion limit of CCM
[
4
]
and well-posedness for the defocusing CCM on a constant background
[
14
]
.

Following the construction of finite-time blow-up solutions in
[
32
]
, Kim-Kwon
[
31
]
established a long-time resolution result for generic
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
solutions with no Hardy space assumption. Their result provides a list that contains all possible asymptotic behaviours for both global and finite-time blow-up solutions. See also
[
29
]
for a construction of blow-up solutions for focusing CCM with a different rates.

On the circle
\mathbb{T}
, CCM (
1.8
) has also been recently studied.
The global well-posedness in
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{T})
was proved by Badreddine
[
2
]
for small (large) data in the (de)focusing setting. The method is based on an explicit formula for solutions in the Hardy space coming from the completely integrable structure.
In the companion paper
[
10
]
, we consider the circle setting for INLS (
1.1
) and prove local well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s>\frac{1}{2}
, establish the infinite depth limit
h\to\infty
, and show unconditional uniqueness in the energy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
.

1.1.
Main results

Our goal in this paper is to make the first steps in investigating the low-regularity well-posedness for INLS (
1.1
) beyond the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
result of de Moura-Pilod
[
17
]
and without relying on complete integrability.
We make the first step towards bridging the gap in the well-posedness theory for (
1.1
), towards the scaling critical space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, where the best previous result is only known for CCM (
1.8
) in the Hardy space
[
30
]
.
We point out that an approach exploiting complete integrability appears to lose its effectiveness in this setting. Indeed, the conservation laws lose their coercivity outside of the Hardy space irrespective of the defocusing/focusing nature of the nonlinearity!
For example, the momentum

\displaystyle\textstyle{P(u)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}iu\partial_{x}\overline{u}+\frac{\beta}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{4}dx}

(1.11)

is a conserved quantity for INLS (
1.1
). For CCM in
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
P(u)
is coercive since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}iu\partial_{x}\overline{u}dx=\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{1/2}}^{2}
.
However, outside of the Hardy space or for INLS, this is no longer true. We instead take a Fourier analytic approach, which has the additional advantage of being applicable to perturbations of CCM and INLS, such as (
1.1
) with
\gamma\neq 0
.
We now state our first main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
s>\frac{1}{4}
. Then, for any
0<h\leq\infty
and
\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, (
1.1
) is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
.
More precisely, for any
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
, there exist
0<\delta\ll 1
and
T=T(\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}})>0
and a unique solution
u
to (
1.1
) in the space

\displaystyle C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))\cap L^{4}_{T}W^{s,4}_{x}\cap(X^{s-\frac{1}{4}-11\delta,\frac{1}{2}}_{T}+X^{s-1,1}_{T}),

(1.12)

satisfying
u(0)=u_{0}
and

\displaystyle v=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}(e^{i\beta F[u]}u)\in X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}_{T}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}u\in X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}_{T},

(1.13)

where
F
is a primitive of
|u|^{2}
defined in (
2.5
), the spaces
X^{s,b}_{T}
are the usual Fourier restriction norm spaces defined in (
3.1
), and
\mathbf{P}_{\pm,\textup{hi}}=\mathbf{P}_{\pm}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}
are as in (
2.3
).

Theorem
1.1
extends the known local well-posedness of INLS (
1.1
) to any
s>\frac{1}{4}
. We find this somewhat surprising in view of (a) our Fourier analytic method of proof of Theorem
1.1
and (b) the similarity of (
1.1
) to other physically relevant nonlinear Schrödinger equations with a cubic derivative nonlinearity whose well-posedness study has been stubbornly restricted to
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
and above when using Fourier analytic methods. Firstly,
Ozawa-Tsutsumi
[
49
]
studied the Cauchy problem for the following derivative NLS equation
1
1

1

The linear operator in
[
49
]
is
i\partial_{t}+\partial_{x}^{2}
. We have transformed the equation via
u\to\overline{u}
for easier comparison with (
1.1
).
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2u\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}).

(1.14)

They introduced the gauge transform:

\displaystyle v(x)=e^{i\int_{-\infty}^{x}|u(y)|^{2}dy}u(x)

(1.15)

which takes a solution
u
to (
1.14
) to the gauged function
v
, which solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v=i|v|^{4}v.

(1.16)

Thus, the gauge has completely ameliorated the derivative nonlinear term!
They then established local well-posedness for (
1.16
) and hence (
1.14
) in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, they also observed that the bilinear form
\partial_{x}(\overline{f}g)

has a certain null structure related to the bilinear Strichartz estimate:

\displaystyle\big\||\partial_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}(\overline{e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\phi}\cdot e^{it\partial_{x}^{2}}\psi)\big\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

(1.17)

This observation will be crucial to us for the case of (
1.1
) as we discuss in the next section. Later, Takaoka
[
56
]
considered the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u),

(1.18)

which is
L^{2}
-critical.
By distributing the derivative, one sees that (
1.18
) has two kinds of nonlinear terms:
u^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{u}
and
|u|^{2}\partial_{x}u
. As described in
[
56
]
, the Fourier restriction norm method can handle the former term but is inapplicable for the latter one. Thus, by employing the gauge transform of
[
49
]
, the latter term can be removed and local well-posedness could be established for (
1.18
) in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
using a contraction mapping argument. This barrier of regularity stood for over 20 years until the complete integrability of (
1.18
) was used in
[
26
]
to prove the global well-posedness in the critical space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We refer to Remark
1.5
for a further comparison between (
1.1
) and our result in Theorem
1.1
and (
1.18
).

Lastly, we point out the connection of (
1.1
) to another interesting cubic derivative-type nonlinear Schrödinger equation, which is the Kinetic DNLS (KDNLS):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(|u|^{2}u)-\beta\partial_{x}[\mathcal{H}(|u|^{2})u],

(1.19)

where
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that (
1.19
) reduces to (
1.18
) if
\beta=0
.
The nonlinearity in KDNLS looks very similar to that in (
1.4
), but there is a crucial difference: for (
1.19
), the parameter
\beta
is real-valued, while (
1.4
) is (
1.19
) with
\beta=-i
. Indeed, when
\beta>0
, (
1.19
) has a dissipative structure as the
L^{2}
-norm of solutions is decreasing. This dissipative structure can be exploited to study (
1.19
) even below
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
. In particular, the effect is stronger on
\mathbb{T}
, where global well-posedness holds in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for any
s>\frac{1}{4}

[
33
,
35
]
. On the line, it is more difficult to exploit the dissipativity. We refer to
[
34
]
for a priori bounds in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
s>\frac{1}{4}
and asymptotic behaviour of solutions under a decay condition
[
36
,
37
]
.

We point out that the space (
1.12
) where the solution lives in Theorem
1.1
simplifies if we additionally assume that
u_{0}\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
when
h=\infty
and
\gamma=0
.
Moreover, the second property in (
1.13
) becomes trivial since
\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}u=0
. We discuss this further in the Subsection
1.2
.

We also discover a Lax pair for INLS (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}
for
\gamma=0
(see (
6.3
)), which differs from that in
[
52
]
and appears to be new in the literature.
When
h=\infty
and
u\in L^{2}_{+}
, our Lax pair reduces to the one that has been used for CCM in
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
19
,
22
,
30
]
.
However, outside of the Hardy space, we are only aware of the papers
[
13
,
31
]
which provide Lax pairs for CCM.
In order to make sense of our Lax operator outside of the Hardy space, we need to assume that the potentials belong to
L^{4}(\mathbb{R})
, which by Sobolev embedding leads to the restriction of potentials in
H^{\frac{1}{4}}(\mathbb{R})
.
We use the Lax pair to establish low regularity a priori bounds in a manner inspired by
[
30
]
.
In order to close a bootstrap argument, we will need to assume that the initial data has small
L^{2}
-norm. We then can then globalise our solutions under such an assumption of small
L^{2}
-norm.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\frac{1}{4}<s\leq 1
,
0<h\leq\infty
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma=0
. Then, there exists
r>0
such that for any
A\geq 0
, there exists
B>0
so that all (global)
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
-solutions to (
1.1
) satisfy

\displaystyle\|u(0)\|_{L^{2}}\leq r\quad\textup{and}\quad\|u(0)\|_{H^{s}}\leq A\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\|u(t)\|_{H^{s}}\leq B,

(1.20)

where
r
and
B
can be chosen uniformly in
1\leq h\leq\infty
.
Consequently, (
1.1
) is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\cap B_{r}(0)
, where
B_{r}(0)=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\,:\,\|u\|_{L^{2}}<r\}
.

We point that the small
L^{2}
-norm assumption cannot be removed in the focusing case for CCM (
1.8
). Indeed, by applying the pseudo-conformal transformation to the static solution
\mathcal{R}
in (
1.10
), one obtains explicit blow-up solutions

\displaystyle u_{\text{sing}}(t,x)=t^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{\tfrac{ix^{2}}{4t}}\mathcal{R}(\tfrac{x}{t})

for all
t>0
. Whilst the profile
\mathcal{R}\in L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
u_{\text{sing}}(t)\notin L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, it can be shown that
u_{\text{sing}}(t)\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for any
0\leq s<\frac{1}{2}
with
M(\mathcal{R})=2\pi
. See also
[
31
]
for blow-up solutions with
M(\mathcal{R})>2\pi
for focusing CCM in the Hardy space.
Above
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, global well-posedness of INLS (
1.1
) (even with
\gamma\neq 0
) with large
L^{2}
-data in the defocusing case follows from conservation of the energy for a suitably gauged version of (
1.1
). See
[
16
]
.

In Section
6
, we provide a further application of our Lax pair by proving that the “polynomial” conservation laws for INLS (
1.1
) (with
\gamma=0
) converge to those for CCM (
1.8
), when
h\to\infty
. See Proposition
6.4
.

By combining Theorem
1.2
with the local-in-time convergence of solutions as
h\to\infty
(even when
\gamma\neq 0
), which follows essentially as a consequence of our proof of Theorem
1.1
, we obtain our third result on the global-in-time infinite depth limit.

Theorem 1.3
.

Fix
s>\frac{1}{4}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma=0
, and
r>0
as in Theorem
1.2
. Let
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\cap B_{r}(0)
and
\{u_{0,h}\}_{1\leq h<\infty}\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\cap B_{r}(0)
a net such that
u_{0,h}\to u_{0}
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
as
h\to\infty
. Then, let
u_{\infty}
and
u_{h}
denote the global solutions to (
1.8
) and (
1.1
), respectively, with
u_{\infty}|_{t=0}=u_{0}
and
u_{h}|_{t=0}=u_{0,h}
constructed in Theorem
1.2
. Then,
u_{h}
converges to
u_{\infty}
as
h\to\infty
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{R}))
.

A similar convergence result holds locally-in-time in the case of large
L^{2}
-norm. We opted to provide a global-in-time convergence statement in Theorem
1.3
for simplicity.

1.2.
Going beyond
H^{\frac{1}{2}}

We now discuss the ideas behind the proof of Theorem
1.1
. We reiterate that the overall source of difficulty and the new ideas used to overcome this are due to the low-regularity below
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
.

The first step is to view INLS (
1.1
) as a perturbation of CCM (
1.8
).
By defining the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{h}:=(\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{T}_{h})\partial_{x}.

(1.21)

we see that (
1.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})-i\beta u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma|u|^{2}u.

(1.22)

To justify this rewriting, we note that there is a strong smoothing effect in the difference
\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{T}_{h}
operator so that
\mathcal{G}_{h}
is
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
-bounded for any
1<p<\infty
. See Lemma
2.4
. For INLS, this observation goes back to
[
17
]
. More generally, the regularising mapping properties of
\mathcal{G}_{h}
have been a key ingredient in the recent progress on the well-posedness for the intermediate long wave equation
\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{T}_{h}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2})

by viewing it as a perturbation of the BO equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2}).

See
[
28
]
, followed by
[
38
,
12
,
11
,
18
,
22
]
.

As our method relies on a Fourier analytic approach and not on complete integrability, we need to perform a gauge transformation to ameliorate the bad interactions in the nonlinear term
u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})
. As discussed further in
[
17
]
, the gauge transform for DNLS in (
1.15
) is not helpful here. Thus, following
[
17
]
, we use a frequency localised version of (
1.15
) given by the new variable
v
in (
1.13
). Frequency localised versions of these gauge transformations originally go back to the work of Tao
[
57
]
for BO.
As shown in Lemma
3.5
,
v
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+\text{l.o.t}.

(1.23)

Like the case of BO and unlike the case of DNLS (
1.18
), the gauge equation (
1.23
) is not closed; it still depends on
u
and the derivative has not been completely removed.
Compare (
1.23
) with the gauged DNLS equation in (
1.16
).
This means that one needs to run a bootstrap type argument (rather than a contraction mapping argument) to construct solutions, juggling both
u
and
v
at the same time, and in the case of INLS (
1.22
), also the third variable
w:=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}u
. In this regard, we follow a similar overall strategy as in
[
47
]
for BO with some additional inputs from
[
25
]
.

The gain in (
1.23
)
comes from having a nonlinearity with a kind of null structure of the type

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\pm}[\mathbf{P}_{\pm}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\mp}\partial_{x}g]

(1.24)

which tames the derivative to an extent:
the first input function
f
has a higher frequency than the second input function
g
and can thus always help to control the derivative
\partial_{x}
. In the case of (
1.23
), this means that, in the
worst
case,
v
and only one of the functions
\overline{u}
or
u
have high enough frequencies, which can be used to control the derivative. In the absence of any
additional
smoothing and regardless of the use of any Strichartz type
L^{p}_{t,x}
-space,
this means that
v
and
u/\overline{u}
must use their derivatives to control
\partial_{x}
. If they can take
s
-derivatives each, this forces
2s\geq 1
, i.e.,
s\geq 1/2
, which roughly shows where the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
barrier appears. Notice that having the Hardy space assumption for the terms
\overline{u}/u
is useless to remove these High-High-Low interactions.

One of the main new ingredients in this paper is the use of a bilinear Strichartz estimate as in (
1.17
), more precisely Lemma
3.2
, in order to gain additional smoothing to weaken the effect of the derivative. In theory, this gives a
1/2
derivative gain which changes the above numerology related to (
1.24
) to
2s\geq 1/2
, i.e.,
s\geq 1/4
. In practice, we are (currently) limited by only being able to use this once with the gauged variable
v
, and it is not available for the functions
u
and
\overline{u}
. Let us explain this further. One of the main steps in the approach of
[
47
]
(and in our case) is a trilinear estimate for the nonlinear term
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]
in (
1.23
) in the setting of the Fourier restriction norm spaces
X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}
of Bourgain
[
7
]
; see Section
4
.
Here, the above heuristics about (
1.24
) are not quite indicative of reality since we also have to control the derivative
\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{s}
from the norm as well as the
\partial_{x}
in the nonlinearity. Eventually though, one will have to rely on the additional smoothing coming from the multilinear dispersion through the phase function
\Phi(\overline{\xi})
and the relation (
4.9
). This is standard fare in the Fourier restriction norm method, although we mention that the phase function here is not strongly non-resonant as is the case for the gauged BO equation, so we rely on a careful case separation to avoid the resonant set.

The moral in the above discussion is that we need to understand
X^{s,b}
-information for the solutions
u
to (
1.22
) and (
1.8
) whose nonlinearities cannot be (entirely) controlled in
X_{T}^{s,\frac{1}{2}+}
. In
[
47
]
, Molinet-Pilod show that smooth solutions to BO belong to
X_{T}^{s-1,1}
, which by interpolation with
X_{T}^{s,0}
, gives control on, say,
X_{T}^{s-\frac{1}{2}-,\frac{1}{2}+}
.
This
X_{T}^{s-1,1}
-property is proved by showing that the nonlinear term
\partial_{x}(u^{2})

belongs to
L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}
. The choice
s-1
is the minimum amount of smoothing needed to control the derivative
\partial_{x}
. For the bilinear estimates for BO, whilst one loses a lot of spatial regularity in placing
u
into
X^{s-1,1}_{T}
, one only does this when
u
has a large modulation which allows to counteract the loss in spatial regularity by a full gain of the phase function.

Here, we arrive at the first issue in using these methods to study CCM (
1.8
) and INLS (
1.1
): these equations cannot be put into a conservative form:
the derivative is embedded into the nonlinearity. For CCM (
1.8
) with the Hardy space assumption, we see from (
1.9
) that the signs
\mathbf{P}_{+}
on the derivative
\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}
and, here is the crucial part, on the first function with frequency
\xi_{1}
, imply that

\displaystyle|\xi_{1}|+|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|=\xi=|\xi|,

(1.25)

so that the output frequency can be used to control the derivative in the nonlinearity and measuring the nonlinearity in
L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}
is effective to show that
u\in X_{T}^{s-1,1}
. This happens in Lemma
3.6
.

However,
this discussion relies heavily on the Hardy space assumption to ensure (
1.25
). We can no longer make this assumption when studying INLS (
1.22
). Consequently, whenever the first input function has a very high
negative
frequency, (
1.25
) becomes
\xi+|\xi_{1}|=|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|

and there are now dangerous interactions of the form

\displaystyle|\xi_{1}|\sim|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|\gg|\xi|.

(1.26)

These are fatal to us below
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
as we would have to rely on the smoothing from at most two input functions forcing
s\geq 1/2
and no amount of weakening the topology to say
L^{2}_{T}H^{s-100}_{x}
is going to help. Our way out of this issue is to decompose
u
into two-parts:

\displaystyle u=u_{g}+u_{b}.

(1.27)

The part
u_{g}
precisely avoids the interactions (
1.26
) and has the desired
X^{s-1,1}_{T}
regularity. Conversely,
u_{b}
contains these bad interactions in (
1.26
) and we instead control it in
X^{s-\frac{1}{4}-,\frac{1}{2}+}_{T}
.
This allows us to use duality and the bilinear Strichartz estimate to recover about half of the derivative and consequently control
u_{b}
even when
1/4<s\leq 1/2
. Moreover, whilst this term has worse temporal regularity, we have gained
2
2

2

The gain is actually better at
\frac{1}{2}-s-
but it is enough for us to use the least amount which is
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}=\frac{1}{4}
.
about
1/4
in spatial smoothing! We detail the precise version of the decomposition (
1.27
) in Subsection
3.4
. The extra spatial smoothing counteracts the worse phase gain when we establish the trilinear estimate (Proposition
4.2
) in the setting of INLS (
1.22
).

We find it interesting that we need extra smoothing from the bilinear Strichartz estimate for proving both the trilinear estimates
and
in establishing the
X^{s,b}_{T}
-property for smooth solutions to the original equation.
We do not believe that the regularity restriction
s>1/4
in Theorem
1.1
is sharp. It seems possible that by implementing additional ideas such as local smoothing estimates and finer decompositions into our approach, the threshold may be lowered to some
0<s_{0}\leq 1/4
. However, such an approach then requires corresponding maximal function estimates, which are difficult to establish below
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
, and it is not currently clear to us if the full sub-critical range could be covered this way.
For the sake of global well-posedness, it is interesting that the threshold
1/4
also appears in making sense of the Lax operator. See also Remark
1.5
for another instance of this numerology.

The remaining of the paper is organised as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce relevant notation, and review properties of the operators
\mathcal{T}_{h}
and
\mathcal{G}_{h}
in (
1.2
) and (
1.21
), and related quantities.
Section
3
introduces the gauged variables
v,w
in (
3.13
), establishes the regularity properties of
u,v,w
for both CCM (
1.8
) and INLS (
1.1
), as well as the finer decomposition needed for the latter (Subsection
3.4
).
The crucial trilinear estimates to handle the nonlinearity are proven in Section
4
.
In Section
5
we present the proof of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.3
on well-posedness of INLS and CCM, and convergence in the limit
h\to\infty
. Lastly, Section
6
presents the new Lax pair for INLS (
1.1
), which is used to obtain the long-time bounds and global well-posedness in Theorem
1.2
, as well as study of convergence of the family of polynomial conservation laws of INLS (
1.1
) in the infinite-depth limit.

We conclude this section with some additional remarks.

Remark 1.4
.

Our result in Theorem
1.1
also extends to suitable perturbations of (
1.8
), such as for the following equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{+}(|u|^{2}u)

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. This equation preserves the Hardy space assumption for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and when
\gamma\neq 0
, we do not know if it is completely integrable. Notice that if
\beta=0
, we get a dispersive version of the Szegö equation
[
21
]
.

Remark 1.5
.

In the following discussion, we compare at a more technical level, the good nonlinearity
v^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{v}
in the gauged version of DNLS (
1.18
) and the derivative nonlinearity in the gauged equation (
1.23
) for INLS (
1.22
).
Consider the spatial multiplier
that naturally appears in the
X^{s,b}
-analysis of
v^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{v}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\xi\rangle^{s}|\xi_{2}|}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{3}\rangle^{s}}\frac{1}{\langle(\xi_{3}-\xi_{2})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(1.28)

where the second factor is the gain of half of the phase function morally coming from the Fourier restriction norm method. In the case when
|\xi|\sim|\xi_{1}|\sim|\xi_{2}|\sim|\xi_{3}|
, the phase function is not helpful to control the first factor, which imposes the condition
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
to control the numerator.
Now consider the nonlinear term
v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|v|^{2})
.
The analogue of Fourier multiplier in (
1.28
) for this term is:

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\xi\rangle^{s}|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{3}\rangle^{s}}\frac{1}{\langle(\xi_{3}-\xi_{2})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(1.29)

Assuming that
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\gtrsim 1
and that the derivative is large, then (
1.29
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\xi\rangle^{s}|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{s}\langle\xi_{3}\rangle^{s}}\frac{1}{\langle\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

In the same nearly resonant situation where all frequencies are similar, we see that the first factor is now controlled precisely as soon as
s\geq\frac{1}{4}
. This heuristically explains the numerology in Theorem
1.1
.
However, in practice, this analogy is not accurate
as the nonlinearity on the gauged side for INLS (
1.22
) is actually
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]
and we do not have the same
X^{s,b}
-properties for
v
and the original solution
u
. Indeed, we only know that
u\in X^{s-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}
and the above numerology does not work.
Consequently, we cannot always gain derivatives through the phase function, and this is where the bilinear Strichartz estimate is helpful.

Remark 1.6
.

Our proof of Theorem
1.1
does not rely on the specific form of the linear operator
\mathcal{G}_{h}
in (
1.21
). In particular, we only need
\mathcal{G}_{h}
to be
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
bounded for
2\leq p\leq 4
and to preserve real-valuedness: if
f
is real-valued, then
\mathcal{G}_{h}f
is also real-valued.
However, at this point, we need the specific form of
\mathcal{T}_{h}
in (
1.2
) to write a Lax pair for (
1.1
), namely the fact that it satisfies a Cotlar-type identity; see (
2.12
). It seems possible to simply use the Lax pair for CCM (obtained by putting
h=\infty
in (
6.3
)) and to use a Gronwall argument to handle the perturbative term in (
2.16
), similar to
[
11
]
. Such a bound would likely not be uniform in time, unlike (
1.20
).
To obtain the convergence result in Theorem
1.3
, we also specialise
\mathcal{G}_{h}
as in (
1.21
).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

In this subsection, we introduce relevant notation, projections, and function spaces, which will be used throughout.

We use
A\lesssim B
to denote
A\leq CB
for some constant
C>0
,

A\ll B
if there is a small
c>0
such that
A\leq cB
, and
A\sim B
if both
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
hold.
The notation
a-
refers to
a-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Also,
a\land b
and
a\lor b
denote the minimum and the maximum between
a
and
b
, respectively.

Given a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}
, we use
\mathcal{F}f
and
\widehat{f}
to denote its Fourier transform

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)e^{-i\xi x}dx.

For space-time functions
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, we may use the notation
\mathcal{F}_{t}u
and
\mathcal{F}_{x}u
to indicate the Fourier transform with respect to the time and space variables. We omit this indexing, when clear from context.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
. We define the
L^{p}
-based Sobolev spaces
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R})
by the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}=\|J^{s}f\|_{L^{p}}=\big\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\big(\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(\xi)\big)\big\|_{L^{p}},

where
J^{s}
denotes the Bessel potential with Fourier multiplier
\langle\xi\rangle^{s}
, where
\langle x\rangle=(1+|x|^{2})
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
stands for the inverse Fourier transform.
We also use
\dot{W}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R})
for the homogeneous Sobolev spaces with norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p}}=\|D^{s}f\|_{L^{p}}=\big\|{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\big(|\xi|^{s}\widehat{f}(\xi)\big)\big\|_{L^{p}},

where
D^{s}
is the Riesz potential, with Fourier multiplier
|\xi|^{s}
.
When
p=2
, we write
W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R})=H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces, with norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}=\|\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(\xi)\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.

We define the inner product on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{f}gdx,

and the dyadic
L^{p}
-spaces, for
1<p<\infty
, via the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\widetilde{L^{p}_{t,x}}}=\bigg(\sum_{N\in\mathbb{Z}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By the Littlewood-Paley square function theorem and Minkowski’s inequality, it holds that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\widetilde{L^{p}_{t,x}}}

(2.1)

for any
2\leq p<\infty
.

Lastly,
given
1\leq p\leq\infty
and an operator
R:L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
, we use
\|R\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}
to denote its operator norm on
L^{p}
.
When
p=2
, we will use the shorthand notation
\|R\|_{\text{op}}
to denote the
L^{2}\to L^{2}
operator norm of
R
.
When working with space-time functions, given
T>0
, we often use the shorthand notation
L^{p}_{T}W^{s,q}_{x}
for
L^{p}([0,T];W^{s,q}(\mathbb{R}))
and
L^{p}_{T}L^{q}_{x}
for
L^{p}([0,T];L^{q}(\mathbb{R}))
.

In the analysis of CCM (
1.8
), we also use the Hardy-Sobolev space
H^{s}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
, defined as

\displaystyle H^{s}_{+}(\mathbb{R})=\big\{f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}):\ \supp\widehat{f}\subset[0,\infty)\big\},

(2.2)

and the Hardy space
L^{2}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
when
s=0
.

We now introduce notation to perform Litlewood-Paley decompositions.
Let
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth function supported on
[-2,2]
and equal to
1
on
[-1,1]
.
Given
N\in 2^{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\eta_{N}(\xi)=\eta(\frac{\xi}{N})
and
\psi_{N}(\xi)=\eta(\frac{\xi}{N})-\eta(\frac{2\xi}{N})
.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{N\geq 1}\psi_{N}(\xi)=1-\eta_{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\quad\text{when }\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

Moreover, we use
\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}
and
\mathbf{P}_{N}
to denote
the Littlewood-Paley projectors defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}f)

\displaystyle=\eta_{N}\widehat{f},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{P}_{1}f)

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}\widehat{f}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}\,\mathbf{P}_{N}f=\mathcal{F}\,\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}f-\mathcal{F}\,\mathbf{P}_{\leq\frac{N}{2}}f=\psi_{N}\widehat{f},\quad\text{when }N\geq 2,

and
\mathbf{P}_{>N}:=1-\mathbf{P}_{\leq N}
. Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{P}_{N}f=f,

which we will often use in our estimates, where by abuse of notation, we assume to sum over dyadic numbers in
2^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
.
We use
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}
for the wider projector with multiplier
\widetilde{\psi}_{N}(\xi)=\psi_{N}(\frac{\xi}{2})+\psi_{N}(\xi)+\psi_{N}(2\xi)
.
We then set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{+}f)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\xi>0}\widehat{f}(\xi),

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{F}\,(\mathbf{P}_{-}f)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\xi<0}\widehat{f}(\xi),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N\geq 2}\mathbf{P}_{N},

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N\geq 8}\mathbf{P}_{N},

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}

\displaystyle=\text{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}},

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}

\displaystyle=\text{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}.

Moreover, we define
\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}=\mathbf{P}_{\leq 2^{-2}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}=\text{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}
, which satisfy
\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}=\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}
. We also define the shorthand
\mathbf{P}_{\pm,\text{hi}}=\mathbf{P}_{\pm}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}
and similarly for
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}},\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}},\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}
.

For space-time functions
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, we define frequency projectors on the space-time Fourier variables
(\tau,\xi)
: given
K\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\{\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}u\}(\tau,\xi)&=\eta_{10^{-10}K}(\tau+\xi^{2})\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi),\\
\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\{\mathbf{Q}_{\gtrsim K}u\}(\tau,\xi)&=(1-\eta_{10^{-10}K})(\tau+\xi^{2})\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi).\end{split}

(2.4)

2.2.
Product estimates

In this subsection, we recall the fractional Leibniz rule and show relevant product estimates involving the function
e^{i\beta\partial_{x}^{-1}|u|^{2}}
which appears in the gauge transformation in Subsection
3.2
.

We will extensively use the fractional Leibniz rule; see
[
15
,
24
,
9
]
.

Lemma 2.1

(Fractional Leibniz rule)
.

Let
s\geq 0
and
1<p_{j},q_{j},r\leq\infty
,
j=1,2
,
such that
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\frac{1}{q_{j}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|J^{s}f\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|_{L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}+\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\mathbb{R})}\|J^{s}g\|_{L^{q_{2}}(\mathbb{R})}.

The gauge transformation from
[
17
]
, which we recall in (
3.13
), requires understanding the function
e^{i\beta\partial_{x}^{-1}|u|^{2}}
. To make this precise, we define the primitive

\displaystyle F=F[u]=\partial_{x}^{-1}(|u|^{2})=\int_{-\infty}^{x}|u(t,y)|^{2}dy

(2.5)

and note that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}F=|u|^{2}.

We then consider the function
e^{i\beta F}
, which satisfies
|e^{i\beta F}|=1
since
F
is real-valued.
It follows that
e^{i\beta F}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Consequently,
e^{i\beta F}
is merely a tempered distribution. Nonetheless, as
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\partial_{x}e^{i\beta F}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, and we have that for almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{x}\{e^{i\beta F}\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{i\xi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}\partial_{x}(e^{i\beta F})dx.

Whilst
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}e^{i\beta F}
,
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}e^{i\beta F}
, and
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}e^{i\beta F}
are well-defined and belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, due to the non-integrable singularity at the origin, the quantities
\mathbf{P}_{\pm}(e^{i\beta F})
are ill-defined. Moreover, we need to carefully define
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{i\beta F}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}e^{i\beta F}
. Here, these are understood as

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{i\beta F}:=e^{i\beta F}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}e^{i\beta F}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}e^{i\beta F}:=e^{i\beta F}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}e^{i\beta F}

and it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{i\beta F})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}e^{i\beta F}-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}e^{i\beta F}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{i\beta F})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}\partial_{x}e^{i\beta F},

(2.6)

where the
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}
appearing on the right-hand side of (
2.6
) agrees with an honest Littlewood-Payley projection to frequencies
\{|\xi|\lesssim 1\}
.

Given
0<\alpha\leq 1
, we have that
D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}
belongs to the subspace of tempered distributions described in
[
5
, Definition 1.26]
. In particular, it follows from
[
5
, Proposition 2.14]
that we have the following equality in the sense of tempered distributions:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}]=D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F},

where the
\{\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}\}_{k}
are homogeneous Littlewood-Payley projectors. In particular, this implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}D^{\alpha}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{i\beta F}&=D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}-D^{\alpha}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}e^{i\beta F}\\
&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}]-\sum^{\infty}_{k=2}D^{\alpha}_{x}\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[e^{i\beta F}]\\
&=\sum_{k<2}\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}]\end{split}

(2.7)

Then,

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}](x)=\int m_{k,\alpha}(x-y)e^{i\beta F(y)}dy=2^{k(1+\alpha)}\int m_{1,\alpha}(2^{k}(x-y))e^{i\beta F(y)}dy,

where
m_{1,\alpha}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big\{|\cdot|^{\alpha}\psi\big\}
and
m_{k,\alpha}=2^{k(1+\alpha)}m_{1,\alpha}(2^{k}\cdot)
,
and thus

\displaystyle\|D^{\alpha}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{i\beta F}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\sum_{k<2}\|\dot{\mathbf{P}}_{k}[D^{\alpha}_{x}e^{i\beta F}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\sum_{k<2}2^{k\alpha}\|m_{1,\alpha}\|_{L_{x}^{1}}\lesssim 1.

(2.8)

Similarly, we have the difference estimate

\displaystyle\|D^{\alpha}_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}[e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

(2.9)

We will need a product estimate involving products of functions with the exponential factors
e^{i\beta F}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
0\leq s\leq\frac{2}{3}
. Given
F_{j}
,
j=1,2
, two real-valued functions such that
\partial_{x}F_{j}=|f_{j}|^{2}
with
f_{j}\in L^{4}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{3}(\mathbb{R})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[e^{iF_{1}}g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\big\{\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|g\|_{L_{x}^{3}}\big\}+\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L_{x}^{2}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[(e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}})g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big[(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}})\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}+(1+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2})\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\Big]

\displaystyle\hphantom{XX}\times(\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L_{x}^{2}}+\|g\|_{L^{3}_{x}}).

(2.11)

Proof.

We begin with (
2.10
). We write
g=\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g+\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g
and consider the contribution coming from each of these parts. First, the projections imply
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[e^{iF_{1}}\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g]=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}(e^{iF_{1}})\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g]
and thus by the fractional Leibniz rule and Bernstein’s inequality,

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[e^{iF_{1}}\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\sim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[(\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}e^{iF_{1}})\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}e^{iF_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s-1}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}(|f_{1}|^{2}e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}(|f_{1}|^{2}e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2}\|\mathbf{P}_{\ll 1}g\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

Now we consider the contribution from
\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g
. By the fractional Leibniz rule (Lemma
2.1
), and (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{iF_{1}})\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

Similarly, by fractional Leibniz, (
2.8
), and Sobolev’s embedding, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{iF_{1}})\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g]\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{6}_{x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{3}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|D^{s-1}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(|f_{1}|^{2}e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{6}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{3}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|D^{s-\frac{2}{3}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(|f_{1}|^{2}e^{iF_{1}})\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{3}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|D^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\gtrsim 1}g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2}\|g\|_{L^{3}_{x}},

given that
s\leq\frac{2}{3}
. We obtain (
2.10
) from combining the estimates above.

For (
2.11
), we apply the same argument as above but with (
2.9
) and the following estimate:

\displaystyle\big\||f_{1}|^{2}e^{iF_{1}}-|f_{2}|^{2}e^{iF_{2}}\big\|_{L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\||f_{1}|^{2}-|f_{2}|^{2}\big\|_{L^{2}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}})\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{4}_{x}}^{2}\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}.

By the mean value theorem, we have
\|e^{iF_{1}}-e^{iF_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}
and this completes the proof of (
2.11
).
∎

2.3.
The operators
\mathcal{T}_{h}

and
\mathcal{G}_{h}

We recall some known facts about the operators
\mathcal{T}_{h}
in (
1.2
) and
\mathcal{G}_{h}
in (
1.21
).
The operator
\mathcal{T}_{h}
satisfies the following Cotlar-type identity: for sufficiently nice
f,g
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}[f\mathcal{T}_{h}g+g\mathcal{T}_{h}h]=\mathcal{T}_{h}f\cdot\mathcal{T}_{h}g-fg-M_{f}M_{g},

(2.12)

where
M_{f}:=\frac{1}{2h}\int_{\mathbb{R}}fdx
.

When
|\xi|\ll 1
,
\coth(\xi)\approx\frac{1}{2\xi}
,
so the Fourier multiplier for
\mathcal{T}_{h}
in (
1.3
) behaves like an antiderivative but it is singular unless applied to functions whose Fourier transform is vanishing sufficiently fast near the origin.
By subtracting this antiderivative term from
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, we obtain a better behaved operator. More precisely, we consider the singular integral operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{h}f(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2h}\text{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg[\coth\bigg(\frac{\pi(x-y)}{2h}\bigg)-\textup{sgn}\bigg(\frac{\pi(x-y)}{2h}\bigg)\bigg]f(y)dy,

(2.13)

for which the following holds.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
0<h<\infty
and
1<p<\infty
. Then,
\mathcal{K}_{h}
is
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
bounded and the operator norm is uniformly bounded in
h
for
1\leq h<\infty
.

Proof.

The kernel
K_{h}
of the integral operator
\mathcal{K}_{h}
can be shown to be a Calderón–Zygmund convolution kernel (see
[
23
, Proposition 5.4.4]
) and thus the claimed
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
boundedness follows. Moreover, it satisfies the scaling property

\displaystyle K_{h}(x)=\tfrac{1}{h}K_{1}(\tfrac{x}{h})\quad\text{for all}\,\,h>0

and thus it satisfies the conditions of
[
23
, Proposition 5.4.4]

uniformly over
1\leq h<\infty
.
∎

For the operator
\mathcal{G}_{h}
in (
1.21
), the presence of the derivative
\partial_{x}
ameliorates the singularity at the origin. Thus, the symbol of
\mathcal{G}_{h}
acts like the identity near the origin and is exponentially decaying. By the Mikhlin-Hörmander multiplier theorem
[
23
, Theorem 6.2.7]
, we then obtain:

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
0<h<\infty
and
1<p<\infty
. Then,
\mathcal{G}_{h}
is
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
bounded and it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{h}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\tfrac{1}{h},

(2.14)

where the implicit constant is uniform in
1\leq h<\infty
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
2.3
. We simply note that the kernel
G_{h}
of the integral operator
\mathcal{G}_{h}
satisfies the scaling relation:

\displaystyle G_{h}(x)=\tfrac{1}{h^{2}}G_{1}(\tfrac{x}{h})\quad\text{for all}\,\,h>0.

The integral operator with kernel
\tfrac{1}{h^{2}}G_{1}(\tfrac{x}{h})
is then a Calderón-Zygmund operator and is
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
bounded, for
1<p<\infty
, uniformly in
1\leq h<\infty
. The extra factor of
h^{-1}
then accounts for its appearance in (
2.14
).
∎

In INLS (
1.22
), we have two kinds of harmless cubic terms: the local one
|u|^{2}u
and the nonlocal one
u\mathcal{G}_{h}(|u|^{2})
. We can deal with these terms simultaneously by defining the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{h}:=-i\beta\mathcal{G}_{h}+i\gamma\text{Id}.

(2.15)

Then, (
1.22
) can be written more succinctly as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u=2\beta u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})+u\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2}).

(2.16)

It is clear that
\mathcal{Q}_{h}
has at least the same
L^{p}\to L^{p}
mapping properties as
\mathcal{L}_{h}
.

3.
The gauge transform and properties of solutions

3.1.
Fourier restriction norm spaces

For
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider the Fourier restriction norm spaces
X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
as the completion of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
under the norm
[
7
]
:

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\big\|\langle\tau+\xi^{2}\rangle^{b}\langle\xi\rangle^{s}\widehat{u}(\tau,\xi)\big\|_{L^{2}_{\tau,\xi}}.

(3.1)

Given a time interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we define localised in time versions of these spaces as follows: if
u:I\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, then

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{I}}:=\inf\{\|\widetilde{u}\|_{X^{s,b}}\,:\,\widetilde{u}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C},\,\,\widetilde{u}|_{I\times\mathbb{R}}=u\}.

(3.2)

When
I=[0,T]
for some
T>0
, we use the notation
X^{s,b}_{I}=X^{s,b}_{T}
.
For any
b>\frac{1}{2}
, the following embedding holds:

\displaystyle X^{s,b}_{T}\hookrightarrow C([0,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{R})).

(3.3)

We recall the following linear estimates related to the Fourier restriction norm spaces. See
[
47
]
, for example.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
T>0
, and
\eta
denote a smooth time-cutoff.

(i)
The following estimate holds

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)S(t)f\|_{X^{s,b}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s}},

where
S(t)
denotes the linear Schrödinger propagator
e^{-it\partial_{x}^{2}}
.

(ii)
Let
0<\delta<\frac{1}{2}
. Then,

\displaystyle\bigg\|\eta(t)\int_{0}^{t}S(t-t^{\prime})g(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\bigg\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|g\|_{X^{s,-\frac{1}{2}+\delta}}.

(3.4)

(iii)
Given
-\frac{1}{2}<b^{\prime}\leq b<\frac{1}{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{X^{s,b^{\prime}}_{T}}\lesssim T^{b-b^{\prime}}\|u\|_{X^{s,b}_{T}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathbf{1}_{[0,T]}u\|_{X^{s,b^{\prime}}}\lesssim T^{b-b^{\prime}}\|u\|_{X^{s,b}}.

(3.5)

(iv)
Given
0\leq\delta<\frac{1}{8}
, the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}-2\delta-}\|u\|_{X_{T}^{0,\frac{1}{2}-2\delta}}.

(3.6)

Proof.

The properties in (i)-(iii) are standard. See for example
[
58
]
.

To show (
3.6
), we first recall the
L^{6}_{T,x}
-Strichartz estimate

\displaystyle\|S(t)f\|_{\widetilde{L^{6}_{T,x}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}},

which by transference principle (see
[
58
, Lemma 2.9]
) and (
3.2
) implies

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\widetilde{L^{6}_{T,x}}}\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{0,b}_{T}}

for any
b>\frac{1}{2}
. Interpolating with the trivial
L^{2}_{T,x}
-estimate gives

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\lesssim\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{4}+}_{T}},

at which point (
3.6
) follows from applying (
2.1
) and (
3.5
).
∎

We now state the bilinear Strochartz estimate.

Lemma 3.2

(Bilinear Strichartz estimate
[
8
,
49
]
)
.

Let
N\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
,
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\eta
be a real-valued smooth cutoff function. Then,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[\eta(t)S(t)f\cdot\eta(t)\overline{S(t)g}]\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}\|g\|_{L^{2}_{x}}.

(3.7)

Moreover, for any
0<\delta\ll 1
sufficiently small, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[u\cdot\overline{v}]\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}+10\delta}\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{2}-2\delta}}\|v\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{2}-2\delta}}.

(3.8)

Proof.

We include a proof of (
3.7
) for the reader’s convenience. Taking the space-time Fourier transform, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,x}\{\eta(t)S(t)f\cdot\overline{\eta(t)S(t)g}\}(\tau,\xi)

\displaystyle=\int\widehat{f}(\mu)\overline{\widehat{g}(\mu-\xi)}\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}(\tau-\mu^{2}+(\mu-\xi)^{2})d\mu.

Note that since
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then, by Plancherel’s theorem and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in
\mu
, we have

\displaystyle[\text{LHS}\eqref{bilin}]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{N}\int|\widehat{f}(\mu)|^{2}|\widehat{g}(\mu-\xi)|^{2}\int|\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}(\tau-\mu^{2}+(\mu-\xi)^{2})|d\tau d\xi d\mu

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{N}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}},

where

\displaystyle M_{N}

\displaystyle=\sup_{|\xi|\sim N,\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\int|\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}(\tau-\mu^{2}+(\mu-\xi)^{2})|d\mu

\displaystyle=\sup_{|\xi|\sim N,\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\int|\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}(\tau+\xi^{2}-2\xi\mu)|d\mu

\displaystyle\sim N^{-1}\sup_{|\xi|\sim N,\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\int|\mathcal{F}_{t}\{\eta^{2}\}(\tau+\xi^{2}-\mu)|d\mu\lesssim N^{-1}.

This proves (
3.7
).
To obtain (
3.8
), on the one hand, (
3.7
) and the transference principle (it is well-known that the linear version of this in
[
58
, Lemma 2.9]
generalises with the same proof ideas to multilinear operators) imply

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[u\cdot\overline{v}]\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}}\|v\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{2}+}}.

(3.9)

On the other hand, by Bernstein’s, Hölder’s, and Sobolev inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[u\cdot\overline{v}]\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\cdot\overline{v}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\|v\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\lesssim N^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{4}+}}\|v\|_{X^{0,\frac{1}{4}+}}.

(3.10)

Interpolating (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) yields (
3.8
).
∎

Remark 3.3
.

The conjugation and the projection
\mathbf{P}_{N}
are important in (
3.7
), and ensure that the constant on the right-hand side of (
3.7
) is essentially independent of the frequency supports of the functions
f
and
g
. If we remove the projection
\mathbf{P}_{N}
, the estimate is now insensitive to any conjugations, and we need to impose a condition on the distance between the Fourier supports of the functions
f
and
g
. Namely, suppose that
f
and
g
are compactly supported on the Fourier side taking values in the sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, respectively. Let
d(S_{1},S_{2})
denote the distance between the sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
:

\displaystyle d(S_{1},S_{2})=\inf_{s_{j}\in S_{j},j=1,2}|s_{1}-s_{2}|.

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)S(t)f\cdot\eta(t)S(t)g\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\lesssim d(S_{1},S_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}.

Finally, we have the following useful
L^{p}
-boundedness for the modulation operators
\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}
in (
2.4
). A similar version of this result was a key ingredient in
[
25
]
. See
[
25
, Lemma 4.6]
. For the reader’s convenience, we detail a proof.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
1<p<\infty
, and
N,K\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
K\gg N^{2}
. Then, there exists
C_{p}>0
, depending only on
p
, such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C_{p}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}f\|_{L^{p}_{t,x}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(3.11)

Proof.

Let
m(\tau,\xi):=\widetilde{\psi}_{N}(\xi)\eta_{10^{-10}K}(\tau+\xi^{2})
be the Fourier multiplier associated to the Fourier multiplier operator
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}
denotes the wider projector and
\mathbf{Q}_{\ll K}
is as in (
2.4
). The
L^{p}\to L^{p}
boundedness then follows from the Marcinkiewicz multiplier theorem
[
23
, Corollary 6.2.5]
once we show that

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha_{2}}m(\tau,\xi)|\lesssim|\tau|^{-|\alpha_{1}|}|\xi|^{-|\alpha_{2}|}

(3.12)

for any
\tau,\xi\neq 0
and multiindex
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
with
|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|\leq 2
.

Note that since
|\xi|\sim N
and
K\gg N^{2}
, it holds that
|\tau|\lesssim K
. Then, we compute:

\displaystyle|\partial_{\xi}m(\tau,\xi)|

\displaystyle\sim N^{-1}|\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}(\tfrac{\xi}{N})\eta_{10^{-10}K}(\tau+\xi^{2})|+\frac{|\xi|}{K}|\widetilde{\psi}_{N}(\xi)\eta^{\prime}(\tfrac{\tau+\xi^{2}}{10^{-10}K})|\lesssim|\xi|^{-1},

\displaystyle|\partial_{\tau}m(\tau,\xi)|

\displaystyle\sim K^{-1}|\widetilde{\psi}_{N}(\xi)\eta^{\prime}(\tfrac{\tau+\xi^{2}}{10^{-10}K})|\lesssim|\tau|^{-1},

where
\widetilde{\psi}:=\widetilde{\psi}_{1}
.
Proceeding in the same way, we obtain the estimates for the second derivatives and conclude that (
3.12
) holds for
m
.
∎

3.2.
Gauge transformation

Following
[
17
]
,
we define the gauged variables

\displaystyle v:=\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{hi}}[e^{i\beta F[u]}u]\qquad\text{and}\qquad w:=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}u,

(3.13)

where
F[u]
denotes the primitive of
|u|^{2}
as in (
2.5
).
For CCM (
1.8
) in the Hardy space, we simply have
w\equiv 0
.
We establish the equations for the gauged variables
v
and
w
in (
3.13
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
T>0
,
0<h<\infty
, and
u\in C([0,T];H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}))
be a smooth solution of (
2.16
).
Then, the variables
v
and
w
defined in (
3.13
) satisfy the following equations:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{v}(u)=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})],

(3.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}w+i\partial_{x}^{2}w

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{w}(u)=2\beta\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}[w\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{hi}}[u\mathcal{Q}_{h}(|u|^{2})],

(3.15)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{h}
is as in (
2.15
).

Proof.

We follow the proof in
[
17
, Lemma 3.4]
. Using (
3.13
) and writing
F=F[u]
for simplicity, we compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v&=\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u]+i\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u]\\
&=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}i\beta(\partial_{t}F)u]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}(\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u)]-\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}\beta(\partial_{x}^{2}F)u]\\
&\hphantom{X}-i\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}\beta^{2}(\partial_{x}F)^{2}u]+2i\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[\partial_{x}(e^{i\beta F})\partial_{x}u]\\
&=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}i\beta(\partial_{t}F+i\partial_{x}^{2}F-\beta(\partial_{x}F)^{2})u]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}(\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u)]\\
&\hphantom{X}-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}(\partial_{x}F)\partial_{x}u].\end{split}

(3.16)

Meanwhile, using (
2.5
), (
2.16
), the fact that
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and that the operator
\mathcal{G}_{h}
in (
1.21
) preserves real-valuedness, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F

\displaystyle=i(u\partial_{x}\overline{u}-\overline{u}\partial_{x}u)+\beta|u|^{4}

and hence

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F+i\partial_{x}^{2}F-\beta(\partial_{x}F)^{2}

\displaystyle=2iu\partial_{x}\overline{u}.

Inserting the above and (
2.16
) into (
3.16
), we find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v

\displaystyle=2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}\{u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})-|u|^{2}\partial_{x}u-u^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{u}\}]

\displaystyle\hphantom{X}+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}|u|^{2}u]-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\mathcal{L}_{h}(|u|^{2})].

Noting that
u\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})=u^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{u}+|u|^{2}\partial_{x}u,

and using (
1.5
),
we further obtain

\displaystyle 2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}\{u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})-|u|^{2}\partial_{x}u-u^{2}\partial_{x}\overline{u}\}]

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})].

Moreover, using that
\mathbf{P}_{+}\mathbf{P}_{-}=0
and
\mathbf{P}_{+}[\mathbf{P}_{-}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{-}g]=0
, we have

\displaystyle-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[v\,\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})].

Here, the second term vanishes since
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{-}g]=0.

Then, we rewrite as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+i\partial_{x}^{2}v

\displaystyle=-2\beta\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[v\mathbf{P}_{-}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+i\gamma\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[e^{i\beta F}|u|^{2}u]-i\beta\mathbf{P}_{+,\textup{hi}}[e^{i\beta F}u\mathcal{L}_{h}(|u|^{2})].

This establishes (
3.14
).

To obtain (
3.15
), we simply apply
\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}
to both sides of (
2.16
) and noting that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[w\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]+\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{lo}}u\cdot\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[w\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})],

since
\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}[\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{lo}}f\cdot\mathbf{P}_{+}g]=0,

completing the proof.
∎

Lastly, from the definition of the gauged variables in (
3.13
), we have the following recovery formula for the solution
u
to (
2.16
):

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}u+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}u+w+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{i\beta F}ue^{-i\beta F}]+w+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,hi}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+w+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}u.

We now apply
\mathbf{P}_{\text{HI}}
to both sides, recalling that
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}=0
, to obtain:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u&=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\\
&\hphantom{X}+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}w.\end{split}

(3.17)

Note that in the second term, the projectors
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}
allow us to place for free an extra projector
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}
onto the first factor
e^{-i\beta F}
.
3
3

3

This can be justified pointwise since the function
u
and hence
F
are smooth, from which we have

\displaystyle\int\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\phi dx

\displaystyle=\int e^{-i\beta F}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}^{-1}[\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{HI}}\phi]dx

\displaystyle=-\int\partial_{x}e^{-i\beta F}\cdot\partial_{x}^{-1}[\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{HI}}\phi]dx

\displaystyle=-\int\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}e^{-i\beta F}\cdot\partial_{x}^{-1}[\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)\mathbf{P}_{-,\textup{HI}}\phi]dx

\displaystyle=\int\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\phi dx

for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.3.
CCM regularity properties: using the Hardy space assumption

First, we establish the
X^{s,b}
-regularity of solutions
u
to CCM (
1.8
). The relatively simple form of the estimate is entirely thanks to the Hardy space assumption.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
s\geq s_{0}>\frac{1}{4}
,
0<T\leq 1
, and
u
be a
H^{\infty}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
-solution to
CCM
(
1.8
) on
[0,T]
. Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq 1}\|u\|_{X^{s-\theta,\theta}_{T}}&\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}+\|J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\\
&\qquad+T^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}.\end{split}

(3.18)

Proof.

We argue as in
[
47
, Proposition 3.2]
.
The main point is that for a suitable extension
\widetilde{u}
on
[0,T]
of
u
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{u}\|_{X^{s-\theta,\theta}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}u+i\partial_{x}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}.

Thus, from (
1.8
), we need to estimate

\displaystyle\|u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})\|_{L^{2}_{T}H^{s-1}_{x}}.

By dyadic decomposition, we focus on controling

\displaystyle N^{s-1}\big\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\cdot\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{N_{23}}[\overline{\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}u}\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u]]\big\|_{L^{2}_{T,x}}.

(3.19)

Since
\mathbf{P}_{+}u=u
, the frequencies, which satisfy
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}
, additionally imply

\displaystyle|\xi|=|\xi_{1}|+|\xi_{3}-\xi_{2}|.

(3.20)

Therefore,
N\gtrsim N_{1}\vee N_{23}
and it is clear that
N_{23}\lesssim N_{2}\vee N_{3}
.

\bullet

Case 1:

N\sim N_{1}
.

By Hölder and Bernstein inequalities, we have

(
3.19
)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{s-1}N_{23}N_{1}^{-s}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}}u\cdot\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-1}N_{23}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}\vee N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}\wedge N_{3}}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{N_{23}(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}-s_{0}}}{N(N_{2}\vee N_{3})^{s_{0}}}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}\vee N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}\wedge N_{3}}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}.

(3.21)

Now we consider the dyadic prefactor in (
3.21
). As
s_{0}>\frac{1}{4}
, we may write

\displaystyle\frac{N_{23}(N_{2}\wedge N_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}-s_{0}}}{N(N_{2}\vee N_{3})^{s_{0}}}\sim\frac{N_{23}}{N(N_{2}\vee N_{3})^{2s_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}}.

(3.22)

If
N_{2}\vee N_{3}\gtrsim N
, then we use that
N\gtrsim N_{23}
to control and
N_{23}
and we have a negative power of the largest dyadic which allows us to sum over all of the dyadics. If instead
N_{2}\vee N_{3}\ll N
, then we further bound by

\displaystyle\eqref{CCMXsbd1}\lesssim N^{-1}(N_{2}\vee N_{3})^{\max(\frac{5}{4}-2s_{0},0)}\lesssim N^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{0}\geq\frac{5}{8}\}}+N^{-(2s_{0}-\frac{1}{4})}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{4}<s_{0}<\frac{5}{8}\}},

where we have a negative power of the largest dyadic frequency, in either case, allowing us to sum in all dyadics.

\bullet

Case 2:

N\gg N_{1}
.

By (
3.20
), we must have
N\sim N_{23}\gg N_{1}
. In particular,
N_{2}\vee N_{3}\gtrsim N
.
We then have two further cases depending on the size of
N_{2}\wedge N_{3}
.

\bullet

Case 2.1:

N_{2}\wedge N_{3}\gtrsim N
.

In this case, we then have
N_{2}\sim N_{3}\gtrsim N
. We follow the argument in Case 1 but placing
\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}}u
into the space
L^{\infty}_{T,x}
while
(N_{2},N_{3})
both go into
L^{4}_{T,x}
, with
\mathbf{P}_{N_{2}\vee N_{3}}u
taking the higher
s
-derivatives. This leads to the dyadic factor

\displaystyle\frac{N^{s-1}N_{23}N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}-s_{0}}}{N_{2}^{s}N_{3}^{s_{0}}}\lesssim\frac{N^{s}N_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}-s_{0}}}{N_{2}^{s}N_{3}^{s_{0}}}\lesssim N_{2}^{\max(\frac{1}{2}-s_{0},0)-s_{0}}\sim N_{\max}^{-s_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}\}}+N_{\max}^{-(2s_{0}-\frac{1}{2})}\mathbf{1}_{\{\frac{1}{4}<s<\frac{1}{2}\}},

which allows us to sum in the dyadics.

\bullet

Case 2.2:

N_{2}\wedge N_{3}\ll N
.

This case is finer since we only have one large input frequency. By symmetry, we will assume that
N_{3}\sim N\sim N_{23}\gg N_{1}\vee N_{2}
. We place
\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u
into
L^{4}_{T,x}
with
s
-derivatives and we want to place
\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}\wedge N_{2}}u
into
L^{\infty}_{T,x}
.
Then by Hölder and Bernstein as before, we have

(
3.19
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\frac{N}{N_{3}}\Big)^{s}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\cdot\frac{N_{23}}{N}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}\vee N_{2}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}\wedge N_{2}}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(\frac{N}{N_{3}}\Big)^{s}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\cdot\frac{N_{23}}{N}\cdot\frac{(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{\max(\frac{1}{2}-s_{0},0)}}{(N_{1}\vee N_{2})^{s_{0}}}\|J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}\vee N_{2}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\|\mathbf{P}_{N_{1}\wedge N_{2}}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{0}}_{x}}.

Now since
s_{0}>\frac{1}{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(N_{1}\wedge N_{2})^{\max(\frac{1}{2}-s_{0},0)}}{(N_{1}\vee N_{2})^{s_{0}}}\lesssim(N_{1}\vee N_{2})^{-(2s_{0}-\frac{1}{2})}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{0}<\frac{1}{2}\}}+(N_{2}\vee N_{3})^{-s_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}\}},

which is a negative power and allows us to perform the dyadic sums over
(N_{1},N_{2})
.
For the sum over
N_{23}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{N_{23}\lesssim N}\frac{N_{23}}{N}\lesssim\frac{N}{N}\sim 1.

It only remains to sum in
N\sim N_{3}
, for which by Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{N\gg 1}\bigg(\sum_{N_{3}\sim N}\bigg(\frac{N}{N_{3}}\bigg)^{s}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{N_{3}}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\bigg)^{2}&\sim\sum_{N\gg 1}\bigg(\sum_{|j|\leq 2}2^{-sj}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{2^{j}N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}\bigg)^{2}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{|j|\leq 2}\sum_{N\gg 1}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{2^{j}N}u\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\lesssim\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}u\|^{2}_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}},\end{split}

(3.23)

which completes the estimate when
N\sim N_{3}
.

Note that in the above arguments, we do not necessarily always close the estimates with
J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u
in
\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}
. Up to adding more factors of
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}
, this causes no issue since by writing
u=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{LO}}u
and using Bernstein’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s_{0}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\leq\|J^{s_{0}}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}+CT^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}_{x}}.

This accounts for the presence of terms such as the third one on the right-hand side of (
3.18
). We apply this comment throughout the rest of the article without further explicit mention.
∎

We make a few remarks about Lemma
3.6
and the proof above. First, we only used the Hardy space assumption for the first factor in
u\mathbf{P}_{+}\partial_{x}(|u|^{2})
not for the factors
\overline{u}\cdot u
. This ensured that (
3.20
) held true so that the output frequency could always be used to control the derivative. This will no longer be the case for INLS (
2.16
) as we discuss in the next section.
Second, the regularity restriction
s>\frac{1}{4}
in Lemma
3.6
can be improved down to, at least,
s>0
by additionally using
L^{6}_{T,x}
. However, including this extra space becomes a nuisance later as we can no longer gain any factor of
T
in the equivalent version of (
3.6
). As the latter arguments rely on
s>\frac{1}{4}
, notably Proposition
4.1
, we opted for a unified presentation.

Lemma 3.7

(Estimates for CCM)
.

Let
\frac{1}{4}<s<\frac{3}{4}
,
0\leq T\leq 1
, and
u
be a
H^{\infty}_{+}(\mathbb{R})
-solution to (
1.8
) on
[0,T]
, and
v
the gauge variable in (
3.13
). Then, it holds that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\lesssim T^{\frac{1}{4}-}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}^{2})\|v\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}_{T}}+T^{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}^{3}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}^{2}).\end{split}

(3.24)

Moreover, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, there exists
\theta>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}_{x}}\lesssim\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}_{x}}+\|v\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}_{T}}&+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}^{2}\big\{TM^{3}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}\\
&+M^{-\theta}(1+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\frac{1}{4}+}_{x}}^{3}+\|v\|_{X^{s,\frac{1}{2}+\delta}_{T}})\big\}.\end{split}

(3.25)

Proof.

We first prove (
3.24
).
By the recovery formula in (
3.17
), recalling that
w\equiv 0
for (
1.8
), and triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{HI}}u\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}&\lesssim\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}+\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}(e^{-i\beta F})\mathbf{P}_{+,\text{lo}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\\
&\hphantom{XX}+\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}\mathbf{P}_{-,\text{hi}}(e^{i\beta F}u)]\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\\
&=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}+\text{I \hskip-8.25128pt I \hskip-8.25128pt I}.\end{split}

(3.26)

We begin by estimating
I
, which we write as

\displaystyle\|J^{s}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}[e^{-i\beta F}v]\|_{\widetilde{L^{4}_{T,x}}}\sim\bigg(\sum_{N\gg 1}N^{2s}\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{+}[e^{-i\beta F}v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We split
e^{-i\beta F}=\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{-i\beta F}+\mathbf{P}_{\textup{hi}}e^{-i\beta F}
and consider each contribution, beginning with the contribution from
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}e^{-i\beta F}
. In the following, after we make use of the outer projection factors such as
\mathbf{P}_{\textup{+,HI}}
to enforce a large output frequency and some frequency sign behaviour we then remove them using their
L^{p}\to L^{p}
boundedness for any
1<p<\infty
. We will do this without further explicit mention.

By duality,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{N}[\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F})v]\|_{L^{4}_{T,x}}=\sup_{\|g\|_{L^{4/3}_{T,x}}=1}\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\mathbf{P}_{N}g}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F})vdxdt\bigg|.

Then, for fixed
g\in L^{4/3}_{T,x}
of unit norm, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\mathbf{P}_{N}g}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{i\beta F})vdxdt\bigg|\leq|\langle\mathbf{P}_{N}g,\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F})(\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}v)\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}|

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt+|\langle\mathbf{P}_{N}g,\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F})(\mathbf{P}_{\gg N}v)\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}|+|\langle\mathbf{P}_{N}g,\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F})(\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}v)\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}|

Then, by an integration by parts and (
2.6
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F}),(\mathbf{P}_{N}g)\overline{(\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}v)}\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F}),\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}[{(\mathbf{P}_{N}g)}\overline{(\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}v)}]\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}(e^{-i\beta F}),\partial_{x}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}[{(\mathbf{P}_{N}g)}\overline{(\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}v)}]\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}\partial_{x}(e^{-i\beta F}),\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}[{(\mathbf{P}_{N}g)}\overline{(\mathbf{P}_{\ll N}v)}]\rangle_{L^{2}_{T,x}}

\displaystyle=0,

since
\widetilde{\mathbf{P}}_{N}\mathbf{P}_{\textup{lo}}=0
for
N\gg 1
. For similar reasons, we see that
